#2926. Is Anthropic Worth $1.4 Trillion? Here's What Investors Have to Believe

Anthropic is set for a splashy IPO. Is it worth buying?

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 18, 2026 at 10:10AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic, PBC ›

Anthropic's (ANT +0.00%) IPO is all set for Thursday, and the AI lab is going to make history.

While it won't top Space Exploration Technologies as the biggest IPO ever, which was valued at $1.75 trillion, it will come in at a close second at $1.4 trillion. What's more important is that it will mark a major milestone in the AI revolution.

Anthropic will be the first AI lab to go public, and by doing so, it will become the clearest representation of the success of AI itself. Up until this point, investors haven't had a good way to invest in companies like Anthropic and OpenAI that are making the products that have come to define AI.

Investors have been able to invest in stocks like Nvidia and other chipmakers that are known as AI stocks, but with Anthropic going public, the AI mantle will be passed.

So is Anthropic a good investment at an IPO valuation of $1.4 trillion? Here's what has to go right for it to work.

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei on stage at a conference. Image source: Anthropic.

Demand for AI compute continues to outstrip the available supply, but the long-term demand for generative AI capabilities isn't fully clear, though it's expected to grow. We're already seeing a backlash against the technology in the form of data center protests that shifted the midterm election results, fears of widespread job loss, and concerns that AI could become destructive, as Anthropic has called for a slowdown in AI development and stronger safety guardrails several times.

Right now, Anthropic is delivering blockbuster growth, but a number of factors could derail that, including political regulations, an AI infrastructure bubble bursting, or a rogue AI incident. While technological progress in AI is likely to move forward, it's not guaranteed that Anthropic and its chatbot peers will be widely embraced in the end.

Anthropic dialed down its target IPO valuation from $2 trillion to $1.4 trillion, in part because of concerns about its massive purchase commitments for chips, AI compute capabilities, and lease obligations.

Anthropic over the next several years owes about $900 billion in purchase commitments, many of which are non-cancelable.

In order for the stock to move higher, Anthropic will have to demonstrate that it is capable of meeting those obligations, ideally through positive cash flow, and that will require significant growth from the company.

CEO Dario Amodei has said before that one of his biggest challenges is knowing how much compute the company needs. We'll find out whether the company has overextended itself or hasn't secured enough compute capacity in the coming years.

The biggest competitive threat to Anthropic may be open-source and open-weight models, which have the potential to undercut Anthropic and siphon away its user base.

While open models aren't expected to match frontier models from Anthropic or others in terms of technological capabilities, they are already taking cheaper work, allowing organizations to avoid paying for expensive frontier tokens.

Dario Amodei has advocated for oversight on open models to ensure authoritarian governments don't achieve military superiority over the U.S., and said the U.S. should crack down on distillation, or open models essentially training themselves from established models.

As Anthropic grows in the coming years, investors should keep an eye on the open model development, as it has the potential to challenge Anthropic and upend the AI race.

Overall, while Anthropic faces a lot of risks in the coming years, the company is also growing rapidly and reasonably valued. It's a good bet to pop on Thursday.

#2922. Is Micron Next to Announce a Stock Split?

The memory chip maker has not split its shares since 2000.

By Will Healy – Nov 18, 2026 at 10:15AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Micron Technology ›

Micron Technology's (MU -0.63%) market performance has investors thinking about something that has not occurred since the dot-com boom: a stock split. The company split its stock three times, with the last one occurring in May 2000.

Now, amid the AI boom and a price that was, until recently, above $1,000 per share, some investors are considering such a scenario again.

Indeed, one cannot dismiss the possibility of Micron's first stock split in more than 26 years. Nonetheless, I think it probably will not be the next stock to split, and here's why.

As of the time of this writing, Micron stock trades at around $735 per share. That makes it the 28th most expensive stock in terms of nominal price trading on U.S. markets today.

On the surface, that makes the chip stock an excellent candidate for a stock split, and investors should not be surprised to see such a move. Additionally, the 259% revenue growth in fiscal 2026 (ended Sept. 3) and the 107% increase forecast for fiscal 2027 suggest that the stock price could rise further.

However, given the state of its peers, it has no pressing need to make such a move. Some of the more prominent tech stock splits in recent years were Apple and Amazon. As components of the price-weighted Dow Jones Industrial Average, they face stock split pressure that Micron does not.

Furthermore, among tech stocks outside of the "Magnificent Seven," peers such as ASML Holding and Sandisk sell at higher nominal prices. Even though a stock can split at any time a company's board sees fit, the next stock split is more likely to come from one of these companies.

Another critical consideration is Micron's historical volatility. As previously mentioned, Micron split in May 2000. Soon after, Micron's pricing power disappeared when demand for its memory chips cratered, and the stock lost nearly all of its value.

This is concerning since analysts forecast that Micron's revenue growth will slow to just 16% in fiscal 2028. Investors tend to punish stocks with slowing revenue growth, and history suggests that could bode poorly for the stock.

Moreover, investors should not assume that the extreme cyclicality of the memory chip industry has disappeared. Indeed, Micron has locked its customers into long-term contracts, which should mitigate the effects of an industry slowdown.

However, given the historical risks of a falling stock price, investors should not expect Micron to rush to any stock split decision.

Under current conditions, Micron is more than likely not the next stock to split, and even if it does, it will likely do so cautiously.

Admittedly, Micron looks like an excellent stock split candidate on the surface, and that could lead to such a move. Its price has recently exceeded $1,000 per share, and larger, more prominent stocks have split their shares in recent years.

Nonetheless, 27 stocks are currently more expensive than Micron, and few of those are in the tech sector. Additionally, given its historical volatility, investors cannot rule out the possibility of a massive pullback. Such conditions probably mean it will approach the stock split issue carefully, if at all.

#2916. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. The agencies conducted a joint review of the 2025 resolution plan submissions from 15 banking organizations with over $250 billion in assets.

In addition, the agencies determined that the shortcoming previously identified in the 2021 BNP Paribas resolution plan has been satisfactorily addressed.

#2911. Despite the loss, Rockets Alperen Sengun continues his career-best shooting

When the Houston Rockets traded for Alperen Sengun in the 2021 NBA draft, they knew they were getting one of the best international players. An MVP of the Basketball Super League at 19, Sengun was already dominating overseas professional leagues.

Over the years, Sengun has gone from an up-and-coming young star to one of the best players in the NBA and a two-time All-Star. Throughout his NBA career, Sengun has also been one of the best post players in the NBA. He has shown he can score in the paint against the NBA's best defenders. Even with his scoring ability in the post, his offensive game has at times been limited by struggles not only in the mid-range but also from the 3-point range.

NBA teams are smart; they have multiple scouts and entire departments dedicated to knowing every strength and weakness of their opponents. Opposing teams quickly realized that if Sengun gets to his spot in the paint, it's usually too late to stop him. So teams started sagging off Sengun on pick-and-rolls, and whenever he got the ball away from the basket, they forced him to take a shot from outside.

That defensive strategy made life a lot harder for Sengun at times, as it became every opposing team's strategy when facing the Rockets. Sengun shot just 24.8% from the 3-point range in his first season, on 1.6 attempts. The next season, Sengun shot a higher percentage at .333 but at half the volume of the first season, as he sometimes hesitated on wide-open 3-pointers, choosing to pass on them and drive into a crowded paint.

Sengun's 3-point shooting dropped over the next two seasons from .333 to a career-worst .233 in his fourth season. Sengun has often said that is the one part of his offense that needs to improve if he wants to unlock his entire game. Sengun worked tirelessly on his 3-point shooting every summer, every practice, and before every game. Sengun has talked for years about his confidence in shooting 3-pointers, as he told SB Nation three years ago during training camp.

The hard work is starting to pay off in his sixth season, as Sengun is off to the best 3-point shooting start of his career.

HOUSTON, TEXAS – APRIL 24: Alperen Sengun #28 of the Houston Rockets shoots a three pointer during the first quarter of the game against the Los Angeles Lakers at Toyota Center on April 24, 2026 in Houston, Texas. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Kenneth Richmond/Getty Images) | Getty Images

Sengun, now in his sixth season, is starting to translate all the hard work he puts into his 3-point shot into the game. Not only has Sengun improved his outside shooting, but he leads the Rockets in 3-point shooting at 48.1 percent. It is on only 2.1 attempts per game, but that is also a career-high for Sengun. A high volume of shots from downtown has never been Sengun's goal; it is more about him being a threat from long range, which opens up the court for the Rockets' center. It's not just that he is making 3-pointers; it's that he isn't hesitating from deep when teams play him for the drive.

Sengun has made a 3-pointer in a career-high six straight games and in eight of his last ten games. For Sengun, if he keeps improving from deep, teams will have to respect him from that range, which gives him more space; with his ability to handle the ball and his court vision, it could take his offensive game to an entirely different level.

With a reliable 3-point game and improved play on the defensive end, Sengun could be in serious contention for MVP votes at the end of the season. Pretty good for a player 16 other teams passed on in 2021.

#2910. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2906. Dillon Brooks’ intensity remains essential to the Suns’ identity

There are plenty of things in this world that you love 85% of the time and can't stand the other 15%. Group texts immediately come to mind. You love being part of the conversation, whether it's with family members, lifelong friends, or your sports buddies discussing the latest developments surrounding the Phoenix Suns. But sometimes those conversations go completely off the rails, your phone starts vibrating like it's sitting on a washing machine during the spin cycle, and you find yourself eternally grateful for whoever developed the technology to mute group text notifications.

As it pertains to the Phoenix Suns, the same could be said about Dillon Brooks.

We were baptized in the Dillon Brooks experience a season ago, and the overwhelming majority of the returns were positive. He's a culture setter and an attitude definer, somebody whose presence dictates the level of physicality with which your basketball team operates. Last season, he also showed us that he has considerably more tools in his offensive arsenal than many of us anticipated, averaging a career-high 20.2 points on 17.1 field-goal attempts per game.

But with that tenacity comes an edge, and navigating it without going over can be a delicate exercise. That's where the other 15% of the Dillon Brooks experience resides. The technical fouls. The flagrant fouls. The moments when the very characteristics that make him such a valuable member of your basketball team become the source of your frustration. And oftentimes, it's the timing of those infractions that makes them particularly maddening. Late-game situations and high-leverage possessions are instances in which discipline needs to supersede emotion, and Brooks doesn't always recognize when that transition needs to occur.

You love what he brings to your basketball team 85% of the time. The other 15%? You find yourself wishing Dillon Brooks had the same mute button as your group texts.

That being said, we've seen a different version of Dillon Brooks thus far this season. He's averaging 13.8 points on 12.0 shot attempts per game. Maybe it starts with the haircut, but whatever has changed, it's manifesting itself in his performance on the basketball court.

With the acquisition of Miles Bridges, Brooks has looked somewhat out of place to begin the season (as has Bridges). Much akin to the duplicative nature of Devin Booker and Jalen Green's skill sets, the same can be said of Brooks and Bridges. They occupy similar spaces on the floor and are asked to perform similar functions within the starting unit. Be a quality point-of-attack defender, knock down the three ball when called upon, and provide some physicality and assistance on the glass.

The Suns have found themselves in an interesting conundrum as the season has unfolded. There are instances when neither player appears entirely certain of where he should be positioned, whom he should be covering, or how to navigate switches on the defensive end. That uncertainty creates a particularly frustrating defensive dynamic because it compromises the very aggression that makes both players effective.

When they're aggressive, miscommunication leaves somebody exposed, and the opposition makes them pay for it. And then there are possessions when it appears they're being overly cautious, almost hesitant to do anything for fear of doing it wrong. That too burns them defensively. For two players whose effectiveness is predicated in large part on their physicality and assertiveness, indecision can be debilitating. It's difficult to impose your will on a basketball game when you're still trying to figure out where you're supposed to be.

This conundrum has contributed to the challenges the starting unit has faced throughout the early stages of the season. They're not a horrible offensive unit, primarily because Devin Booker's consistency has served as the rising tide that lifts the boats around him. Defensively, however, this group has yet to establish an identity. And when you juxtapose that against what we're experiencing from the second unit, a group that clearly understands its individual roles and how those roles complement one another, it becomes glaringly apparent how discombobulated the starting five has been.

Last night against the Denver Nuggets, we saw head coach Jordan Ott make an adjustment that appeared to unlock Dillon Brooks. That adjustment? He opened the second quarter with Brooks alongside members of the second unit, and almost immediately we saw Dillon being Dillon again.

Through his first three minutes as part of that lineup, Brooks attempted four shots. Granted, he went 1-of-4 from the field, but what stood out to me was the decisiveness with which he was operating. There was a familiarity to his approach, an aggressiveness that we'd grown to appreciate a season ago. He looked empowered to assert himself offensively without having to navigate the overlapping responsibilities that exist within the starting five.

The shots weren't necessarily falling, but the mentality was there. And from that point forward, Brooks delivered one of his best performances of the young season.

Now, I'm not about to head to my group text and flood its participants with messages advocating for Dillon Brooks to take 17 shots a night again. As Brooks has shown us throughout his career, his inefficiency can be his downfall. He attempted more shots than any member of the Phoenix Suns last night, going 6-of-17 from the field to get his 15 points. Not exactly great.

What this team needs more of is an engaged Dillon Brooks. Somebody who's emotionally invested in the game, actively disrupting what the opposition is attempting to accomplish, and embracing the alpha mentality that has become synonymous with his brand of basketball.

Brooks is the emotional leader of the starting unit, much like Jordan Goodwin is the emotional leader of the second unit. When Brooks is passive and isn't asserting himself, it affects his individual performance and, by extension, the performance of those around him. His intensity has a trickle-down effect on his teammates, and when that intensity is absent, you can feel it throughout the lineup.

Again, it's a fine edge to navigate. But it's one Brooks has navigated throughout his NBA career. It's his X factor, the characteristic that makes him special and gives him value beyond his statistical production.

Hell, he even picked up a horseshit technical foul last night after knocking down a three-pointer and barking at the opposing bench. Apparently, only Dillon Brooks can earn a technical foul for doing something we routinely see across the NBA. As frustrating as the whistle was, it was another indication of how engaged he was in the game.

It was his third technical foul of the season through 13 games, meaning he's averaging one every 4.3 games. That's a slightly more disciplined pace than a season ago, when he averaged one every 3.3 games en route to accumulating a league-leading 17 technical fouls.

The ideal balance, in my eyes, is a version of Brooks who takes somewhere around 11 shots per game, assumes greater offensive responsibility when sharing the floor with the second unit, and remains the emotional leader of the starting five. Defensively, you want him to take charge, communicate, dictate physicality, and impose his will, rather than operate with uncertainty and defer to those around him.

There's no perfect formula for finding that balance, particularly for a player whose greatest strength can occasionally become his greatest weakness. The Suns need Brooks to understand when to turn the volume up and when to dial it back. But they also need him to remain the emotional heartbeat of that starting unit. His aggression, physicality, and willingness to get under somebody's skin are part of what makes him valuable.

Give me an engaged Dillon Brooks, and I'll live with having to mute the group text from time to time. Now do me a favor, send this article to one of your friends via group text.

#2905. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2902. Dan Campbell sends Lions clear celebration message after Aidan Hutchinson injury

With the Lions losing star edge rusher Aidan Hutchinson for the season in their 35-10 overseas win over the Patriots, head coach Dan Campbell stood firm on his stance regarding player celebrations.

Hutchinson's season came to an unfortunate end after a sack on Patriots quarterback Drake Maye in the first quarter; the five-year veteran landed awkwardly after jumping in the air in celebration and tore his ACL in the process.

During Tuesday's appearance on Detroit's 97.1 The Ticket, Campbell verified the defensive star's injury status and how it will affect an already depleted defense.

Detroit Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson (97) sits on the field after an injury during a game in Munich, Germany, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. AP Photo/Matthias Schrader

"What a gut punch, just because … he's such a phenomenal player," Campbell said. "This guy's an impact player, but more importantly, he's an unbelievable teammate, and he's one of our captains.

"It just stinks, man. It hits you. And I hate it for him, man, because he was on a heater. What a hell of a year he was having. He's just improved every year and to come off the injury he had [in 2024]. But I also know this, man: this guy will overcome this and he will come back better than he was, too. It's just the way he is, it's the way he's wired, and he'll come out better for this."

Regarding whether the celebration caused the injury, Campbell played it off as a freak accident.

"No, I don't believe that," Campbell said. "I think what happened speaks for itself. We're not talking to 8-year-olds here. And I'll give you a story. I remember one time I made a big play and I freaking pumped my arm up and I'm pretty sure I tore my bicep, but I didn't tell anybody. I was just excited. I just pumped my arm. It's not like I — so if you say, 'Hey, don't pump your arm next time, make sure you stay stoic,' man, this game is built off of emotion. And if you have to contain yourself after a play is made … then you're really becoming something you're not."

Detroit Lions head coach Dan Campbell speaks at a press conference after an NFL football game in Munich, Germany, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

The gesture was similar to the typical goal celebration in soccer that fans of FC Bayern Munich would see at Allianz Arena in Munich, Germany, where the latest European NFL matchup was held.

Lions fans remember the outcome vividly, as former linebacker Stephen Tulloch suffered the same fate as Hutchinson back in 2014 against the Green Bay Packers.

Hutchinson had 24 tackles on the season and six sacks before getting hurt.

This marks the defensive end's second season-ending injury in the span of three seasons.

The injury, Campbell said, was "one of those things that happened."

"I hate it, I know he hates it. But I mean, the number of moves that I've seen Hutch make and the number of plyometrics, explosive movements, the spins, the one leg lateral movements, lateral balances, I'm just like, it was one of those things, man," the coach said. "I hate it. I think we all understand what it is, and let's be smart, but no, I'm not gonna say, 'Nobody celebrates anymore.'"

The Lions went into the international matchup without middle linebacker Jack Campbell, who left Week 8's 19-15 win over the Minnesota Vikings on a stretcher.

Campbell is currently on injured reserve with a neck injury.

Celebrations have been a recurring topic in the NFL, and the Lions have been at the center of the conversation, from Amon-Ra St. Brown emulating President Donald Trump's dance to Jameson Williams getting a penalty for dry-humping the turf after a touchdown.

#2891. Hawks' CJ McCollum out at least 4 weeks with calf strain, putting rookie Kingston Flemings in the spotlight

Atlanta Hawks guard CJ McCollum has a left calf strain and will be sidelined for at least four weeks. The Hawks announced the injury update on Tuesday.

McCollum sustained the injury in Sunday's win over the Milwaukee Bucks. Imaging has since revealed the calf strain. He'll be reevaluated in four weeks, per the Hawks.

Through 14 games, McCollum is averaging 14.5 points, 3.9 assists, 3.1 rebounds and 1.1 steals per game while shooting 43.3% from the floor and 35.2% from 3. The Hawks enter Tuesday at 8-6, tied with the Miami Heat for the fourth-best record in the Eastern Conference.

The injury is a blow for the Hawks as they lose a key playmaker and their veteran voice on the court.

McCollum, 35, began the season as Atlanta's starting point guard. But he gave way to rookie Kingston Flemings in the starting unit, recently telling the NBA on Prime that he approached head coach Quin Snyder about making the lineup change.

"I didn't like the way we were playing as a unit, as a starting unit," McCollum said. "I think we were a little sluggish. I think we were lackluster from a ball movement standpoint.

"I think we were stagnant, and we have a lot of guys that can create and make plays. And I think for Kingston, he's a guy who gets downhill. He's got tremendous speed. He's learning and growing. And I think it gives him a chance to kind of develop, for us as a franchise and for us as an organization. …

"The way I play, I can get to it off the bench, I can get to it as a starter. I'm mature enough to be able to handle different roles, different minutes."

With McCollum sidelined, the keys to the offense will belong to Flemings, whom the Hawks selected with the No. 8 pick in June's draft out of Houston. In 12 games including five starts, Flemings is averaging 7.8 points, 4.1 assists, 2.5 rebounds and 0.7 steals in 21.5 minutes per game.

Flemings' shooting efficiency has stood out, especially for a rookie. He's shooting 51.4% from the field and 42.9% on 1.8 3-point attempts per game. He's done so in limited playing time as McCollum has continued to play heavy minutes off the bench.

Flemings has been day-to-day with back tightness, but was reportedly able to practice on Tuesday. He projects to have an opportunity to build on his success with increased volume and playing time in McCollum's absence.

The Hawks also announced Tuesday that second-year backup forward Asa Newell will miss at least four months with a plantar plate tear in his left great toe that required surgery. Newell was averaging 3.6 points and 2.6 rebounds in 11 minutes per game in five appearances this season.

#2890. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2889. Chiefs sign RB E.J. Smith, Emmitt's son, to the practice squad

The Chiefs signed running back E.J. Smith to their practice squad on Tuesday, according to the NFL's personnel notice.

Smith is the son of the NFL's all-time leading rusher, Emmitt Smith.

The Texas A&M product signed with the Chiefs as an undrafted free agent after the 2026 draft. He spent the offseason and training camp with the team but was waived with an injury designation Sept. 4.

He had remained a free agent until Tuesday.

Smith spent four seasons at Stanford and two at Texas A&M. He finished his career with 207 carries for 969 yards and nine touchdowns and added 470 receiving yards and a touchdown.

Smith does not have a jersey number yet on the Chiefs' roster, but he won't wear his father's No. 22. Rookie defensive back Jadon Canady has that number in Kansas City. Smith wore No. 35 in training camp, but practice squad running back Nate Carter has that number.

The Chiefs also signed tight end John FitzPatrick to the practice squad. They terminated the practice squad contracts of wide receivers Jacob DeJesus and Bub Means.

#2887. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2883. Inside the Cowboys historic defensive collapse against the 49ers

The Dallas Cowboys lost a game to the San Francisco 49ers. That's not a shocker to anyone, as this Cowboys team has struggled all season, and the 49ers are the best team they've played so far, and by a good margin. Looking at their opponents, San Francisco is the only offense the Cowboys have faced this year that is ranked in the top eight in scoring, now averaging over 31 points a game. In fact, the Cowboys haven't fared well against offenses that average over 22 points a game.

They've played five teams that average more than 22 points/game. Their record against those teams is 1-4.

They've played five teams that average fewer than 22 points/game. Their record against those teams is 3-2.

Clearly, playing decent offenses is not ideal for this Cowboys defense, so when they faced the NFC's highest-scoring offense, it was a huge problem for a unit that has struggled immensely all season. Even so, it shouldn't have been that bad. The defense gave up 584 total yards on the day. It was the most yards they have ever given up at home during their 67-year existence. The most ever is never good.

The frustrating part about this game is that when you take a closer look, it's easy to tell that the Cowboys never had a shot. The defense was just outmatched. It was like playing one of those games where you play against an older sibling, where they might toy around with you for a bit, only to flatten you when it's time to get serious.

The 49ers did that to the Cowboys' defense. That doesn't mean they were playing opossum at any point during the game. It's just that whenever the 49ers found themselves in a crunch, they always had a go-to play where they knew they could take advantage of the Cowboys' vulnerabilities.

The Cowboys had several defensive breakdowns in the game, with multiple third-down letdowns and explosive plays. One of the most embarrassing plays came on the Deebo Samuel reverse play in the third quarter. When fullback Kyle Juszczyk went in motion, the entire defense was lured into a trance. Follow the Juice because that's where the action goes most of the time. Only this time, the Juice wasn't worth the squeeze.

Sadly, the entire defense took the bait. It was crazy to see how many defenders continued chasing running back Kaelon Black. That left an entourage of blockers out in front of Samuel, leaving very little chance for the Cowboys to make a stop. Malik Hooker and Shemar James were the first to figure it out, but they were so far out of position that by the time they caught up, they were left gambling and taking poor angles. Cornerback Joey Porter Jr. was the last hope. He's not the greatest tackler as it is, but he's even worse when he's washed out by multiple blockers.

In our game preview article, we mentioned that "Shanahanigans" would be an issue due to the Cowboys' over-pursuing nature, and right on cue, they obliged. It was one of the poorest-defended reverse plays we've seen.

Mental lapses are common for this defense, but more frustrating is when their effort is so lackadaisical. The touchdown run by the rookie Black was horrible for different reasons. It starts with Juice looping around with banana motion. He and wide receiver Jordan Watkins take on the outside defenders, leaving a nice hole for Black to run through. With no defenders to impede his path, Black gets a full head of steam and commits some terrible Big 10 rookie-on-rookie crime by running over Caleb Downs.

Yes, that's the Cowboys' top draft pick being leveled, but in fairness to Downs, he squared up well. It's just hard to defy physics when the running back comes plowing through with that much force.

The rest of the Cowboys' defense is sealed off from clever run-blocking that creeps to the second level. Cornerback Cobie Durant has one final chance to bring him down, but he gets run over like he's not even there. It wouldn't be a Cowboys game without a humiliating run-stop attempt against a team's backup running back.

Getting their playmakers in space was the name of the game for the 49ers, and they did it often. They just meandered off to one side, got the defense to cha-cha-cha in that direction, and then boom, sealed off! On the quick pass to Samuel, the Cowboys' defense was so out of position that their lone, helpless cornerback wasn't even able to get a hand on him.

And then there were the two long touchdown passes to Christian McCaffrey. To be fair, McCaffrey is a handful all of the time, so game planning for him isn't easy. The first touchdown catch was especially hard because the defense actually played well for 90% of the play. It was just hanging on for that last 10% that got them in trouble. McCaffrey was a sneaky sneakerton and, at the last second, leaked out into the secondary, and as soon as Brock Purdy saw that, it was over.

The second touchdown was a harder pill to swallow for a couple of reasons. First, it essentially iced the game. Any hopes of a comeback were crushed. Second, in the defense's biggest moment, they were once again escorted out of the play. It was a simple play for the 49ers, with all their receivers basically running away from where McCaffrey was, taking the defense with them. It was like Moses parting the Red Sea, only Kyle Shanahan was holding the staff, and it was the Cowboys' defense that opened up for the easiest pitch-and-catch you'll ever see.

The Cowboys' defense has been a problem all season, and the 49ers exploited every element of it. The same things keep happening, and they aren't showing any sign of improvement. And it's not going to get any easier with four of their final five games against offenses averaging over 22 points a game. This defense has fallen into troubling times with very little chance of escape.

#2882. NBA trade rumors 2026-27: Dejounte Murray market lukewarm, what about Zion Williamson?

NBA front offices generally wait about 20 games to assess their teams before seriously looking into the trade market. Even more, it's not until Dec. 15, when many contracts signed over the summer become trade-eligible, that the market really heats up. That, however, never stops the NBA trade rumor mill, so here is the latest.

Soon after news broke of Dejounte Murray's agent requesting a trade for the point guard out of New Orleans, rumors tried to link Murray to the Phoenix Suns. There was some logic to it: The salaries line up and, at least outside the Valley of the Sun, the sense is Phoenix could use a point guard.

However, most of that speculation seemed to come out of Murray's camp, not Phoenix, which has limited interest in him, according to multiple reports, including Chris Manix here at NBC Sports. He also reports the market for Murray is "lukewarm." Actually, lukewarm may be putting it kindly. Here is what one "high ranking" NBA executive told Kelly Iko of Yahoo Sports:

"What a f****** mess. No one wants to touch him."

Coming off a torn Achilles that cost him most of two seasons, other teams see Murray as a distressed asset making $32.8 million this season, with a player option he almost certainly picks up for $30.8 million next season. Teams will want picks, likely a first-round pick, to take on that much money. Based on the history of Pelicans lead executive Joe Dumars in dealing with Trey Murphy III and Herb Jones (two players other teams actually want), this is going to drag out.

The Sacramento Kings are 1-12 and their coach, Doug Christie, is in the final year of his contract. On 29 teams in the league, that would be the definition of a coach on the hot seat, but in Sacramento they pushed back hard when the idea first came out, saying Christie was safe. He is a favorite of ownership there.

That hasn't completely changed. Marc Stein of The Stein Line reports the Kings "would prefer not to fire" Christie during the season to limit the amount of "tumult and upheaval" during Darius Acuff Jr.'s rookie campaign. Most likely, Christie is let go after the season. If/when that happens, two names to watch are Tom Thibodeau and Mark Jackson. Thibodeau (who has connections to GM Scott Perry) has coached more recently and had more success, while former Warriors coach Mark Jackson is a favorite of owner Vivek Ranadive from those days in Golden State.

In other Sacramento news, they are still interested in adding veteran point guard Gabe Vincent as a backup behind Acuff, he has a standing offer, Stein reports. That he hasn't jumped at the deal could be telling.

• With all the Pelicans trade talk right now, would they consider trading Zion Williamson? Maybe, but the return isn't going to be as robust as they might hope, reports ESPN's Tim MacMahon on the Hoop Collective podcast: "He's somewhere on the LaMelo (Ball) to Ja (Morant) spectrum, in terms of trade value." Ball went for a first-round pick and Naz Reid, plus three pick swaps, and MacMahon is saying Williamson would get a smaller return than that. Morant was traded for Jerami Grant and Kris Murray.

• While the Miami Heat have found a groove of late, this is a team known to be looking for upgrades, and Ira Winderman of the Sun Sentinel suggests to watch out for Zach LaVine as a target.

• The Indiana Pacers may be in the market to trade for a wing, but they want to get Tyrese Haliburton back healthy and let things shake out first, Stein reports.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2877. Nick Bosa’s shocking drought comes with a 49ers silver lining

The San Francisco 49ers hold an 8-1 record, the best in the NFC, and yet they still haven't gotten quite the same type of production out of Nick Bosa that they're accustomed to.

NBC Sports Bay Area 49ers insider Matt Maiocco was asked if Bosa would ever be productive again and provided his insight on why he's a key contributor to the 49ers' pass rush.

"Nick Bosa does not have a sack in the four games he has played this season. Never before has he gone four consecutive games in the same season without a sack since coming into the NFL in 2019," Maiocco said.

San Francisco 49ers edge rusher Nick Bosa during team warmups at AT&T Stadium. Getty Images

Since Week 2 of last year, Bosa has missed an extensive amount of time dealing with a torn ACL last season and a calf injury this year.

Maiocco added that Bosa not being able to participate in training camp this past summer put him behind the rest of his teammates, and then when he was cleared to play again, he played in two games, and then strained his calf.

Bosa returned two weeks ago against the Raiders and recorded one tackle. He played 57 snaps against the Cowboys last Sunday, finishing without a tackle but registering a quarterback hit.

Nick Bosa coming out of the tunnel before an NFL game against the Cowboys. Getty Images

Maiocco added that Bosa is still winning despite the longest drought of his career.

"Bosa leads the 49ers' defensive line with a pass-rush win rate of 16.7 percent. That is down from past seasons, when he typically is around a 20 percent figure," Maiocco said.

On Monday, 49ers head coach Kyle Shanahan appeared on a conference call that included Maiocco, and he was asked if he was worried about Bosa and told reporters, "I'm not worried about Nick."

"Nick is going to get better each week, and he'll eventually get there," Shanahan said.

While San Francisco is waiting for Bosa to reform himself, the 49ers defense can be patient.

Nick Bosa attempting to tackle Raiders tight end Michael Mayer. Getty Images

The 49ers rank second in the NFL in run defense, allowing 82.6 rushing yards per game. They're also giving up an average of 21.8 points per game.

Their pass rush has been inconsistent, especially Sunday, when the 49ers managed just one sack of Cowboys quarterback Dak Prescott, who threw for 343 yards and five touchdowns.

The 49ers are tied with the Eagles for 30th in the NFL with 16 sacks, underscoring their struggles to get after opposing quarterbacks.

Perhaps if Bosa can get back to his old self soon, he could give the 49ers' pass rush the jolt that they desperately need.

#2871. Tuesday Miami Dolphins Thoughts With The Earl

This game really depends upon your outlook for the season. If you are a "get the first draft pick at all costs" person, then you like this outcome. If you want to see a win and positive development, this was a horrible outcome. It was another of the "close, but no cigar" games the Dolphins have played this season. Here's what I saw.

In previous games, there was a tradeoff between the offense and defense over which would show up. In this game, the offense was expected to be worse than the defense, with a backup QB getting his first-ever NFL action. However, the Dolphins still couldn't go a game without some form of tradeoff. In this matchup, it was a tradeoff between the pass defense and the run defense. The Dolphins allowed a completion percentage over 70% and had a run defense that allowed less than 4 yards per carry. The pass defense had its best performance of the season. They held Danny Dimes to 50% completion and a 4.9 YPA average. They picked him off twice, one of which was returned for a TD. They allowed a passing TD and a couple of big plays but were solid in coverage. In a shock to everyone, the Dolphins allowed more rushing yards to TE Tyler Warren (3) than receiving yards (1). Warren was held without a catch until the 4th quarter and was a complete non-factor.

Unfortunately, while the Dolphins pass defense stepped up covering the one receiving threat the Colts had, the run defense couldn't stop the other threat the Colts posed. The Colts rushed for 172 yards on 34 carries, 106 of those yards coming in the 1st half on 19 carries. That's a 5.6 YPA allowed. They improved a little in the second half, allowing 66 yards on 15 carries and a 4.4 YPA. But on the final drive, they allowed 12 yards on 2 carries that finished off the game. Just once, the team needs a complete performance on both sides of the ball. Now they need a complete performance on both sides of the ball. I will give them some due credit. They limited the Colts to 17 offensive points and were hamstrung by a limited offense, but the run defense was the backbreaker in this game. With the injuries mounting on the DL, I don't know how they can fix it.

We'll get to the obvious problem in a moment. Bradley Pinion had 3 punts for 134 yards, with a 55-yarder. That's an average of 44.7 overall and 39.5 on the other two punts. Indy's punter had 6 for 290, averaging 48.3. The league average is 47.5. Miami still allowed too many yards in the kick return game. They had the 15-yard penalty on Louis, though I won't complain about penalties since the total was lopsided in Miami's favor and that penalty was ultimately inconsequential. It is still unacceptable, though.

I said on Friday that the Dolphins simply aren't good enough to win without winning in the margins. This is a young team working to find its identity. They come to work every day and put it all on the line every game. This isn't the 2024 or 2025 Dolphins with players obviously checking out and Tua laughing on the sideline or Hill taking himself out of a game. This is a bad team playing like they are trying to get the top seed in the AFC. When the kicker, the second oldest player on the team, shanks not one, but two FGs and misses a PAT as the icing on the cake, that is a level of terrible that cannot be measured. After I watched the game and went to bed thinking of all I planned to say about how he cannot remain on the team if the coaches wanna keep the locker room, the Dolphins made my job easier and waived him on Monday. So that is a non-issue now.

And I've seen the tweets about how the misses keep the tank alive and all that. But I cannot stress enough how not only is tanking a bad idea in general, but you really only tank for a QB, and the 2027 QB class is buns. They are bad. It's an elevated class due to absences and wouldn't be considered top prospects in a class like 2018 or 2020. I would love for the Dolphins to get Jeremiah Smith as much as any other fan. But this team is NOT trying to lose. They aren't playing like they are trying to lose. They are talent deficient, but they aren't tanking. So when you play as hard as they do and the veteran kicker can't do the basic requirement of putting the ball through the uprights, then a message needs to be sent, and it was.

Kyle McCord's play will be dissected by many others, so I don't want to get deep into it. I thought he played ok. I thought he played like a player getting his first NFL snaps. His performance isn't worth getting worked up over one way or another. I said in Friday's post that a healthy Willis wins this game by 2 scores, and I still believe it. But McCord played well enough for the Dolphins to get a win against another equally terrible team and was let down. The next step will be to see what, if any, level of improvements can be made.

The run game has disappeared, and it probably won't come back for a while. I tried to watch to see how much Indy loaded the box, but I didn't truly keep track. Indy is a bad team, but Miami will face some good teams coming up; teams with solid to great defenses. My guess is they will load the box and force McCord to beat them. Having Meyer in there instead of Brewer hurt as well, but the OL can only do so much. The good news for the RBs is they were involved in the passing game. All three RBs were targeted 9 times with 8 catches for 62 yards, including the 4-yard loss for Kaytron Allen. If the RBs can contribute in the pass game, then the offense can function about as normally as we can expect. Even with the drop-off, Gordon is still on pace for a 1000 yard season.

Caleb Douglas was invisible for most of the game but stepped up late. He will continue to be a force. Washington had the play of the game IMO with the one-handed catch down the sideline. He continues to improve in getting separation and being a factor in the offense. The TD throw was nice also.

The TEs were invisible, and that's a concern. I like Dulcich, and he has potential, but he is playing his way off the roster for 2027.

The passing defense stepped up as I mentioned, and its leaders are two rookies. Michael Taaffe logged his 3rd INT this season, and Chris Johnson got his 2nd in consecutive weeks. It should have been his 3rd in 4 weeks, but that Jets WR fell down and they said Johnson interfered. He was a ballhawk in college, and it is showing up now. There are 17 players in the league with 3 or more INTs and only 7 with more than 3. Taaffe has the instincts to be around the ball a lot. His development going into the rest of this season will be interesting to watch.

Regarding the kicker position, I would not be opposed to the Dolphins adding a young player – an undrafted rookie or second-year player – to fill the position. Vets are what they are, and while they offer some reliability, I'm all for giving a young player a chance to make his mark. They probably won't because, well, they are trying to win games. But if they swing and miss with young players, at least they tried. If they get a hit, that's something they can build on moving forward.

Circling back to the opening paragraph, I am not a fan of losing games for a draft pick. I want the Dolphins to win. This game was frustrating because they had it and gave it away. They lost by the margins and lost the game. Unfortunately, this was the last win on the schedule for a while. Unless McCord magically turns into Tom Brady in the next few games, the Dolphins are looking at 1-16. They host the Jets in 2 weeks, but I don't like that matchup with McCord. They host the hapless Chargers in week 16, and if Willis is back healthy, they'll have a good chance of winning. They have the 8-1 Bills and 7-2 Broncos on the road in 2 of the next 3 games. They get the 7-2 Bears and 6-4 Packers, all battling for playoff seeding and that division title. Perhaps the Bills will lock up home-field advantage by week 17, and the Dolphins will get backups. Otherwise, the outlook is grim. To paraphrase Captain Jack Sparrow: if you were waiting for the opportune moment to get a win… that was it.

We all knew this season wasn't about the wins, but 1-16 is a tough mark for anyone. Even if the Dolphins go 12-5 over the next 2 seasons (they won't), that is still 25-26 over 3 seasons. It's going to be much worse than that. It is going to be a long road back to relevance for this franchise. I'll cover that later on (either this week or next), so stay tuned.

#2866. Jaylen Brown ruled OUT vs. Magic with hamstring strain

#2860. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.
