#2757. Future Eagles Fits: Jeremiah Smith headlines an explosive 2027 WR class

Future Eagles Fits: Jeremiah Smith headlines an explosive 2027 WR class originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Welcome back to Future Eagles Fits, our weekly deep dive into the prospects who could eventually find their way to Philadelphia.

Every week, we're going inside the tape and looking at prospects through an Eagles-centric lens — breaking down the athletic profile, positional versatility and scheme fit that could ultimately determine whether a player makes sense in Philly. Along the way, we'll examine how prospects are being evaluated across the league as draft boards begin to take shape and conversations are had with representatives across the scouting landscape.

For the Eagles, it's been a mixed bag on the outside this fall. DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks have missed significant time, Darius Cooper has shown some flashes albeit in small spurts, but rookie Makai Lemon, who currently leads the team in targets, receptions and receiving yards through the first nine weeks of the season, has done an excellent job all things considered. The first-year wideout has been a focal point in the offense and looks like a legitimate complementary WR2 moving forward. Still, however, there are questions about the overall composition of the group and whether Philly has enough explosiveness on the outside to consistently threaten defenses at all three levels. You know what Smith offers, and Lemon has provided a reliable presence, but adding another dynamic playmaker with the ability to create separation, stretch the field and generate yards after the catch could give this offense another dimension.

Currently, the Eagles hold the fifth overall pick in the 2027 NFL Draft, along with the 25th overall pick following the trade of Jordan Davis to Jacksonville. That gives Howie Roseman unique flexibility at the top of the draft, particularly if Philadelphia wants to make another significant investment in the receiver position after drafting Lemon on Night 1 last spring. Whether it's targeting a potential franchise-caliber weapon with an early first-round selection or finding another explosive playmaker later in the round, there are several intriguing options to consider.

While there is still a ton of football left to be played and the entirety of the pre-draft process still to play out – which will alter draft boards – here are six pass-catchers to know in the upcoming class — two potential Day 1 targets, two Day 2 prospects and two Day 3 options who could provide some pop on the outside.

Day 1: Jeremiah Smith, Ohio State | 6'3″, 225 lbs, estimated 4.43 40 time (via summer testing)

If the Eagles are fortunate enough to have Jeremiah Smith available with the fifth overall selection, sprint the card up to the podium.

Simply put, Smith is as elite a wide receiver prospect that the NFL has seen since the likes of Julio Jones coming out of Alabama years ago. A "potential All-Pro as a rookie" as a source said, his combination of size, physicality, and explosiveness is as impressive as you'll find at the position in this draft class, or any other. At 6-foot-3, he has the ability to overwhelm defensive backs physically while maintaining the athleticism and explosiveness to separate at the top of routes, the strength to consistently win through contact and the type of ball skills that allow him to make plays far outside his frame hauling in passes like a Venus flytrap above the helmet of opposing corners.

A former five-star recruit and Heisman trophy candidate this fall, what separates Smith from other physically imposing receivers in the past is his ability to dominate at all three levels of an offense. He's not simply a big-bodied possession receiver who wins in the red zone. He can threaten defenses vertically, create separation on intermediate routes, and turn underneath opportunities into explosive gains with his ability to run through contact and generate yards after the catch.

There's also a level of polish to his game that makes his evaluation particularly exciting. Smith has the physical tools to dominate NFL corners immediately, but he's also shown the ability to consistently win in different situations and against different coverage looks. He's the type of receiver who doesn't need a perfect quarterback situation to produce, making him about as close to a quarterback-proof offensive weapon as you'll find. Quite frankly, he's a player who could have made the jump to the NFL a couple of years ago and still been one of the more talented receivers in the league. He's that special of a talent.

For the Eagles, Smith would represent an opportunity to add a legitimate cornerstone piece to the offensive skill group and showcase what would be arguably the NFL's top wide receiver tandem alongside DeVonta Smith. With questions surrounding the long-term composition of the receiver room and the potential need for another dominant outside presence, his ability to win vertically, create after the catch and consistently command attention from opposing defenses would immediately change the way teams have to defend the Eagles. If he's available at No. 5, no matter what your depth chart looks like, you turn in his card and don't think twice. He's the top player in the 2027 class.

Day 1: Jackson Harris, LSU | 6'2″, 205 lbs, 4.45 estimated 40 time (via summer testing)

A transfer to Baton Rouge from Hawaii, Harris has gone from being a likely Day 3 pick to an athlete that will likely hear his name called on Night 1.

At his size, Harris brings an appealing combination of outstanding route-running and playmaking ability on the outside. He's a receiver with the type of frame NFL teams covet, particularly when evaluating players who can operate outside the numbers and give quarterbacks a legitimate target in contested situations. His transition to the SEC has been particularly impressive, and the production he's put together this season has helped elevate his draft stock. After announcing himself on the SEC stage in Week 4 with 195 receiving yards against Texas A&M, Harris is coming off of electric back-to-back performances against Auburn and Alabama to the tune of 21 catches for 359 yards and two TDs combined in the last two weeks. Defenses know the ball is going his way, and they still can't do anything about it.

For the Eagles, the 25th overall selection could be an interesting range to explore if Harris remains available. His size and ability to provide another outside presence would give the Eagles some additional flexibility with its personnel groupings.

Day 2: Deuce Alexander, Ole Miss | 5'11", 180 lbs, 4.33 estimated 40 time (via summer testing)

If you're looking for an explosive weapon who can immediately add a completely unique dimension to an offense, Alexander is the guy here on Day 2. A Wake Forest transfer, Alexander brings a completely different physical profile than some of the bigger-bodied receivers at the top of the board as his burst and top end speed will force opposing coordinators to know where he his on every snap.

He's not going to overwhelm defensive backs with size, but his fundamental explosiveness and ability to create separation are what make him such an interesting prospect. There are some similarities to Lions WR Jameson Williams in the way he moves and threatens defenses vertically, as Alexander has the type of acceleration and track speed that can force opposing defenses to respect the deep ball, while his ability to create separation gives him opportunities to make plays underneath, as well. He's the type of receiver who can force advantageous matchups for an offense simply by being on the field.

For the Eagles, Alexander would provide a different element to the receiver room. Philadelphia already has a few established options who can operate in the intermediate areas of the field, but adding another receiver with legitimate vertical speed could open up opportunities for the entire offense by forcing defenses to cover every single blade of grass. His ability to threaten defenses over the top and generate explosive plays would make him an appealing complementary piece, particularly if the Eagles are looking to add more speed without allocating first-round capital.

Day 2: Ryan Wingo, Texas | 6'1″, 214 lbs, 4.37 estimated 40 time (via summer testing)

Wingo is another receiver who makes just about everything look easy when watching the tape. Operating in Steve Sarkisian's offense at Texas, he's been utilized in a variety of ways, and that versatility is one of the more appealing aspects of his evaluation.

At 6-foot-1, Wingo has an impressive combination of size, strength and elite athleticism. He's a well-built receiver who can operate outside, but his skill set extends beyond simply lining up on the perimeter and running traditional route concepts. Texas has done an excellent job finding different ways to get him involved, whether that's through jet sweeps, the screen game, vertical opportunities or allowing him to create after the catch to showcase his track speed (as a former All-State sprinter during his prep years in Missouri).

His versatility is highly intriguing as he doesn't need a specific type of play call to make an impact. He's comfortable working with the ball in his hands, has the speed to threaten defenses vertically, and also possesses the physical profile to create problems when defensive backs attempt to tackle him in space. That alone is unique. He's the type of player who can provide an offense with multiple answers depending on the defensive personnel and coverage they're facing.

There's also something to be said about how naturally he operates within the offense. Wingo doesn't necessarily need to be force-fed opportunities to become an impact player, and his ability to contribute in different areas makes him an appealing evaluation for teams looking for more than just a traditional outside receiver.

For Philly, that versatility could be particularly valuable. The Eagles have continued to look for ways to create more explosive opportunities within its offensive structure, and Wingo would provide another player capable of contributing both on designed touches and as a legitimate downfield threat. His skillset expands a playbook.

Day 3: Cayden Lee, Missouri | 5'10", 175 lbs, 4.55 estimated 40 time (via summer testing)

Don't dismiss Lee because of his size, as his ability to consistently make plays regardless of the situation is what makes him worth monitoring. An Ole Miss transfer, Lee has established himself as one of the most productive receivers in the SEC and currently leads the conference in receiving yards with 835 through nine games.

At 5-foot-10, his game isn't predicated on overwhelming defensive backs physically. Instead, his game is built around nuance – the ability to consistently catch the football, mirroring similar routes, create separation and make plays beyond his frame. He has some of the best hands in the country and understands how to find openings in coverage and give his quarterback an opportunity to complete passes, and there's a level of consistency to his game that stands out when watching him. There are some similarities to Lemon in the way he operates as a receiver, as well. He's not the biggest, fastest, or most physically imposing player on the field, but his ability to consistently get involved, make difficult catches and produce within an offense showcases a heck of a football player.

The Eagles don't necessarily need another receiver who profiles as a traditional outside target, but finding a reliable player who can contribute as a WR4 or WR5 while providing some additional explosiveness to the offense potentially on special teams is never a bad investment. His ability to consistently make plays and his production against SEC competition make him a name worth keeping an eye on as the process unfolds.

Day 3: Justin Bowick, Oklahoma State | 6'3″, 203 lbs, 4.50 estimated 40 time (via summer testing)

Bowick is a name whose draft stock could continue trending upward as the evaluation process moves along. Playing at Oklahoma State and catching passes from Drew Mestemaker, potentially the first QB off the board in April, Bowick has an opportunity to continue building his profile as a big-bodied perimeter target.

At 6-foot-3, he brings a physical profile that immediately pops on tape. He's a large presence on the outside who can present matchup problems for opposing defensive backs, particularly when working in contested-catch situations and giving his quarterback a bigger window to throw into. His size alone makes him an interesting developmental prospect, especially for the Eagles who could eye another outside receiver who can provide a different physical element to their offense.

Right now, he has mid to late Day 3 grades from teams but is likely a player to fully indulge himself in the pre-draft process, meaning a good showing at an All-Star game (Senior Bowl or East-West Shrine), the NFL Combine, and pro day circuit could move him up boards significantly.

#2756. Fever’s Front Office Search Takes Major Turn as NBA Executives Emerge

13 Jun 2000: Allison Feaster #5 of the Los Angeles Sparks dribbles the ball during a game against the Seattle Storm at Key Arena in Seattle, Washington. The Storm defeated the Sparks 59-69. The Indiana Fever are moving closer to finding their next basketball executive, and their search has drawn three candidates with extensive NBA experience.

Boston Celtics vice president Allison Feaster, Sacramento Kings vice president Phil Jabour and former Dallas Mavericks executive Matt Riccardi have emerged as strong candidates for the position, according to Annie Costabile and Sam Amick of The Athletic, who first reported the development Tuesday, Nov. 17.

ESPN's Alexa Philippou and Shams Charania subsequently reported that Indiana is working through a group of finalists, with a hire expected within the next month.

Philippou also clarified that the Fever intend to hire one basketball operations executive rather than separate replacements for former team president Kelly Krauskopf and former general manager Amber Cox.

The search comes at a critical point for Indiana, which must address major roster decisions surrounding Caitlin Clark and Kelsey Mitchell.

The development comes nearly four weeks after the Fever parted ways with two of their highest-ranking executives.

Getty13 Jun 2000: Allison Feaster of the Los Angeles Sparks dribbles the ball during a game against the Seattle Storm at Key Arena in Seattle, Washington. The Storm defeated the Sparks 59-69.

Feaster brings a combination of playing experience and NBA front office leadership.

A former WNBA player, she currently serves as Boston's vice president of team operations and organizational growth. She joined the Celtics in 2019 after an accomplished professional playing career.

Her work has focused on player development, organizational culture and relationships throughout the basketball operation.

The Kings executive has worked extensively in scouting, player evaluation and roster construction. Before joining Sacramento, he held a scouting leadership position with the Philadelphia 76ers.

Riccardi, meanwhile, previously served as an assistant general manager with the Mavericks.

He also spent more than a decade with the Brooklyn Nets organization, including a stint running their G League affiliate.

Following Nico Harrison's dismissal in Dallas in November 2025, Riccardi and Michael Finley briefly shared interim general manager responsibilities.

All three candidates bring NBA experience, although Indiana has not announced a finalist or identified a preferred choice.

The Fever's search began after team president Kelly Krauskopf stepped down Oct. 23.

General manager and chief operating officer Amber Cox departed later that day, leaving Indiana with significant vacancies in basketball leadership.

However, Philippou clarified that the Fever intend to hire one basketball executive rather than separate replacements for Krauskopf and Cox.

The incoming executive will inherit a franchise with championship expectations and several consequential personnel decisions approaching.

Krauskopf helped build the Fever from their inception and guided the franchise through its first WNBA championship in 2012.

Cox, meanwhile, oversaw basketball operations during a period of heightened attention surrounding the team's young core.

GettyKelsey Mitchell and Caitlin Clark of the Indiana Fever confer against the Chicago Sky during the first half at Wintrust Arena on August 23, 2026 in Chicago, Illinois.

The timing of Indiana's search adds urgency to the decision.

Caitlin Clark remains the centerpiece of the franchise, while Kelsey Mitchell enters another important offseason following a career-best 2026 campaign.

Mitchell's upcoming free agency presents an immediate challenge for whoever takes charge.

The four-time All-Star has established herself as one of the WNBA's premier scorers, making her future a central question in Indiana's plans.

The next executive must also evaluate the roster around Clark and Mitchell while working with coach Stephanie White to establish a sustainable championship contender.

Indiana's leadership changes have created uncertainty. Tuesday's report, however, suggests the search is entering its final stages.

With several NBA-connected candidates under consideration and a decision expected within weeks, the Fever could soon have the person responsible for guiding their next chapter.

#2755. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2754. Lakers’ trust in starter’s defensive gambles paying off

Matisse Thybulle's explanation for how he steals passes midair while opponents try to get the ball up the court is simple.

Even if the act of stealing the pass like a free safety intercepts a quarterback isn't.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Matisse Thybulle (4) steals the ball from the Sacramento Kings during an NBA game, Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026, in Sacramento, Calif. AP Photo/Scott Marshall

"I just look at the person passing; I just make eye contact with them," Thybulle said after Tuesday's shootaround ahead of the Lakers' home game against the Hornets. "Because most guys aren't really throwing no-look passes. Like Luka [Dončić], [Nikola] Jokić, there's a couple guys in the league who really throw nowhere passes, especially in transition.

"So if you just look people in their eyes, they're going to look where they're throwing the ball. I can just get a sense from the eye contact."

Matisse Thybulle of the Los Angeles Lakers blocks a shot by Devin Booker of the Phoenix Suns on Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026, in Phoenix, Arizona. Getty Images

But if everyone was able to pick off passes like Thybulle, who's shown a knack for playing the passing lanes throughout his eight-year NBA career, then they would.

So what makes him more equipped at picking off passes in a way that looks easy?

"Just being willing to take the risk," he said. "I've missed a bunch of gambles as well as getting some. So just being willing to take the risk of assuming that where they're looking is in fact where the guy is trying to pass the ball to."

The Lakers have benefited from the defensive gambles the 6-foot-5 wing takes.

They entered Tuesday leading the league in defensive turnover percentage (18.7), according to Cleaning The Glass, and Thybulle is a significant reason.

He entered Tuesday ranked No. 2 in the league in total steals (31) and deflections (60).

Giannis Antetokounmpo shoots against Matisse Thybulle, left, and Austin Reaves during an NBA basketball game Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

"Me and [assistant] coach Greg [St. Jean] have talked a lot about that — just trying to find ways for me to be me within the foundation of our defense," Thybulle said. "And they like me gambling because it generates turnovers, whether I'm getting the steal. It's just a matter of doing it in ways where our defense can absorb the mistake, if you will. If I do it in ways where our defense is structured to be able to absorb it, then it's something that we can just use, and I try to use to give us an advantage."

The Lakers empowering Thybulle to take advantage of his defensive freedom, similar to how Dončić takes advantage of his offensive freedom, goes back to trust that was established during the preseason and training camp.

"It can be challenging at times to play with someone like me who's taking a lot of risks, gambling, getting out of position and sometimes throwing off the team defense," he said. "And it can be really easy for them to just be like, 'Well, Matisse keeps messing up our defense. Like, what the hell is he doing?' But they understand and they let me learn from my mistakes."

He continued: "I gamble and sometimes I don't get the pass, but sometimes I get a steal on the back end. There's just the rapport that's grown through that."

#2753. Tech outages are causing people to panic buy. Costco is benefitting

A series of tech outages has consumers stocking up on emergency supplies.

Costco sales are seeing a lift because of it, according to research firm Mizuho.

In the final week of October, Mizuho estimates Costco's U.S. comparable sales, excluding gasoline, rose 11% to 12%, up from the normal 6% to 7% the first three weeks in October. Costco reported an 8.1% increase for the month. The firm said the stronger pace is continuing through November as 21 of the 26 high-ticket generators it tracked on Costco's website sold out as of Sunday night.

Shares of Costco were roughly flat-to-lower in Wednesday morning trading around $1,107 apiece.

"Consumer anxiety following the Microsoft Outlook outage, the ground stoppage for all U.S. airline flights, and apocalyptic concerns flaring up across social media have all contributed to increased emergency prep sales at COST," said Mizuho analyst David Bellinger in a research note Monday. Bellinger will appear on CNBC's "The Exchange" during the 1pm hour.

The stockpiling intensified from a series of recent disruptions. Microsoft's Outlook and Teams outage on Thursday, the Federal Aviation Administration halted departures nation-wide on Friday. A separate software outage at JPMorgan Chase Monday caused banking transaction delays while a system outage delayed United Airlines flights Monday. The National Security Agency on Tuesday said the spy agency's communication network went down temporarily.

The various outages appear to come amid a rush to recover software vulnerabilities uncovered by AI tools and officials are saying there's no reason for panic.

Still, on TikTok and other social media platforms, posts urging people to stock up on food and supplies are gaining significant traction.

Mizuho raised its price target on Costco to $1,150 from $1,100 and kept its buy-equivalent outperform rating. The firm said U.S. comparable sales could grow more than 10% in November.

Mizuho also sees Walmart's Sam's Club benefiting from emergency preparedness sales as shoppers fill up their pantries, and for Home Depot and Lowe's as they buy home improvement emergency supplies.

At Costco, Mizuho will be watching whether the surge brought in new members sign-ups when the warehouse retailer reports its first quarter of fiscal 2027 on Dec. 10.

#2752. Duke hits new low in 37 years with blowout loss to Illinois

Duke hits new low in 37 years with blowout loss to Illinois appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

History has the receipts proving Duke is a basketball powerhouse. However, nobody is completely invincible.

On Tuesday, No. 5 Duke reluctantly welcomed No. 10 Illinois to the famed Cameron Indoor Stadium in Durham, North Carolina. The Illini defeated the Blue Devils 99-78. It was not only a blowout loss, but also the first of its kind in a long time for Duke, per Joe Tipton of On3Sports.com.

The last time the Blue Devils lost by more than 20 points at home was in 1989, 37 years ago, to be exact. Additionally, the Blue Devils ' 35-game winning streak at home came to an end.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

Illinois came ready to play. A major factor was Andrej Stojakovic scoring a record 46 points, the most points scored by an opponent at Cameron Indoor Stadium since Grady Wallace scored 43 points for South Carolina in 1957. Despite their size and height, Duke was outrebounded by Illinois in a 54-34 win. The Illini also grabbed 18 offensive rebounds and scored 19 second-chance points.

Plus, Illinois won the battle in the paint, 48-30. They also put up 22 points off 14 Blue Devils turnovers. The Blue Devils shot 24-for-64 from the field (37.5%) and 9-for-40 from 3 (22.5%). They were also only 21-for-33 from the field and missed 12 free throws.

For Duke, freshman center Joaquim Boumtje-Boumtje led the way with 17 points and five rebounds. Senior guard Caleb Foster added 15 points and six rebounds. Second-year guard/forward Sebastian Wilkins added 10 points.

The Blue Devils are now 3-2. They enter the season trying to shake off an Elite Eight exit to UConn after being the No. 1 seed. On Saturday, the Blue Devils will play against Utah in Pittsburgh.

#2751. Eagles sign Zach Ertz to 53-man roster in flurry of moves

Eagles sign Zach Ertz to 53-man roster in flurry of moves  originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

It looks like Zach Ertz is going to have a chance to break the franchise record for receptions after all.

The Eagles on Monday afternoon made a flurry of roster moves headlined by signing Ertz from the practice squad to the 53-man roster. There are eight games left in the 2026 season and Ertz is just seven catches behind Harold Carmichael for the franchise receptions record.

Here's an overview of the roster moves from Monday afternoon:

• TE Zach Ertz and OT John Ojukwu signed to 53-man roster

• RB Will Shipley's 21-day practice window opened

• RB Jaydon Blue, LB Chance Campbell, TE Hayden Rucci signed to practice squad

• DB Shaun Wade released from practice squad

The Eagles brought back Ertz to their practice squad on Sept. 22 but used up his three elevations against the Bears, Rams and Jaguars. He has been stuck on the practice squad for the last four games. But coming off the bye week, Ertz is finally on the roster and will have a chance to break that record.

1. Harold Carmichael: 5892. Zack Ertz: 5823. Pete Retzlaff: 4524. Dallas Goedert: 4405. Brian Westbrook: 4266. DeVonta Smith: 4117. Brent Celek: 3988. DeSean Jackson: 3799. Pete Pihos: 37310. Keith Byars: 371

Earlier this season, Ertz played in those three games and had 3 catches for 22 yards. Ertz, 36, is obviously past his prime but did have 50 catches for 504 yards with the Commanders last season before tearing his ACL.

By signing Ojukwu, the Eagles are signaling that he's their new swing tackle. The Eagles released veteran Fred Johnson late last week so Ojukwu is now the next guy up behind Jordan Mailata and Markel Bell. Ojukwu went undrafted out of Boise State in 2023. He played in 16 games with 9 starts with the Titans from 2023-25. Ojukwu joined the Eagles on a futures deal in January and has been on the practice squad all year.

Shipley has been on IR since Oct. 16 with a foot injury. With his practice window now opened, the Eagles will have 21 days to activate Shipley or shut him down for the season. Right now, the only running back on the 53-man roster is Saquon Barkley.

As far as the practice squad transactions, both Blue and Campbell have been with the Eagles earlier this season, but Rucci is a new face. The 25-year-old went undrafted out of Wisconsin in 2024 and has been with a few teams. Earlier this season, he played in six games with two starts with the Chargers.

The Nets have been a bottom five team in the NBA this season from the free-throw line, and they certainly left too many points at the stripe in Monday's loss to the 76ers.

Jaylen Brown of the Philadelphia 76ers dribbles against Julius Randle of the Brooklyn Nets in New York, Monday, Nov. 16, 2026. The 76ers won 135-123. Getty Images

"Obviously I don't want to shoot 64 percent. … If we shoot better, we probably have a bigger chance. But I know our guys are good shooters and they're gonna make free throws. We gotta concentrate and make sure that we believe that all of them are going to go in."

#2749. Trump admin insists recent closely timed tech outages aren't connected

There is no indication that a recent wave of high-profile technology outages is the result of a cyberattack or any form of coordinated action, a senior Trump administration official said Wednesday.

"We have a great deal of confidence in our assessment that these incidents are the result of upgrades of the systems, systems being patched, hardware being applied" or other "disparate" events, the official said on condition of anonymity to discuss the government's intelligence assessments.

The official briefed news outlets by phone in the wake of numerous major disruptions at large corporations and federal agencies in recent days, including a nationwide ground stop, a protracted Microsoft Outlook outage and a reported blackout of the National Security Agency's classified communications network.

Other incidents include software issues at JPMorgan Chase, an outage at United Airlines that caused some flight delays and product issues at Apple.

#2748. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2747. Retail investor enthusiasm for Anthropic IPO to rival SpaceX debut

Retail investor Dan Shetty is so eager to get in on the Anthropic initial public offering that he "drained" his Roth IRA and moved roughly $12,000 from Fidelity to Robinhood for a chance to participate.

"I don't want to tell my grandkids that I didn't invest in Anthropic and I didn't invest in OpenAI during the AI bubble," he said.

Shetty's enthusiasm is shared by many, with excitement among small investors for the blockbuster IPO expected to be off the charts. Vanda Research estimates that Anthropic could see approximately $250 million of retail net buying when it starts trading Thursday, double the size of SpaceX's IPO.

"That would be far and away the largest amount of net retail buying of any stock that we've seen in our data on the first day of its listing," Jai Malhi, senior strategist at Vanda, said in an interview with CNBC.

The artificial intelligence laboratory — which will trade on the Nasdaq under ticker symbol ANT — is selling 825 million shares at an expected price range of $70 to $78 per share in the IPO, though sources told CNBC the stock is likely to price above that range. Individual investors are expected to be allocated as much as 20% in that initial offering. And if Vanda's figures prove correct, the buying enthusiasm will be big as regular trading begins.

Founded less than six years ago, Anthropic aims to raise as much as $64.4 billion, giving it a valuation of $1.405 trillion. If demand is especially strong, Anthropic could even raise more than the record $85.7 billion that SpaceX sold in its record IPO in June.

The tech trade has been in turmoil since Jensen Huang's departure last week as Nvidia CEO raised questions about the future of AI financing. Before then, a potential slowdown in data center development resulting from Democratic victories in the 2026 midterm elections, and signs of moderation in the capital expenditures boom, had already spooked investors.

These all stand in marked contrast to the market backdrop in place at the time of the SpaceX IPO. In June, both positioning and sentiment were high, Malhi said. The Anthropic IPO could see a different dynamic, however. Demand could be strong across the entire investor spectrum given that sentiment is depressed, meaning that the bar to beat expectations is "much easier to overcome," he explained.

"What we're seeing across both retail and institutional positioning is that there is plenty of dry powder to take part in this IPO," the strategist said.

For the 27-year-old New Yorker Shetty, who himself works at agentic data platform Data Workers, this is his first time vying to take part in an IPO. He said Anthropic's emphasis on "safety first" drew his interest.

"I've been telling all of my friends, 'If you have like $5, you should invest it in this stock because this is the pure-play AI leader,'" Shetty said. "I think of my father who invested in Microsoft during the dotcom bubble, and he divested from that stock, and it was such a silly thing to do."

Joe Pak, a former macro portfolio manager who is now an independent researcher outside London, is opting against participating in the Anthropic IPO for two reasons: the likelihood of limited allocation and current macroeconomic conditions.

He believes the odds are probably stacked against retail investors when it comes to the allocation of shares.

"It's very difficult as a retail investor to actually get an allocation in these blockbuster IPOs," the 36-year-old said. "You get maybe one share, and the base case is you don't get any at all. So, it's a bit of a catch-22. If you do get a lot of shares, it probably means that the institutional bid was weaker, and in that case, you don't actually want the shares at that level."

In terms of the economy, conditions "need to be favorable" to hold a position in a stock like Anthropic, he said. But with the Federal Reserve having raised interest rates in September and widely expected to do so again next month, the backdrop could be darkening.

"The risk that you have is the financial conditions deteriorating" before the company starts to grow into anticipated profit and revenue, which would justify purchase of the stock, Pak said.

While Pak is steering clear of the IPO, he did say that at a certain level, it would make sense to get into Anthropic.

Other retail investors such as Bryan Nearnberg share Pak's view.

"I'm planning to eventually buy Anthropic, just not at the IPO price, considering when the lockups expire and there's some selling pressure," said the 37-year-old Westchester County resident, who works in finance operations. "We are not at the end of this short-term negative headline sentiment."

IPOs are a sore spot for Nearnberg, who hasn't had much luck trading stocks on their opening day. His skepticism toward IPOs can be traced all the way back to when he was in college.

"I didn't get an allocation to the Facebook IPO, but I ended up buying it the first day of open. I got burned pretty quickly," Nearnberg told CNBC. "Same thing with Zynga. I didn't get an allocation, but [got] burned pretty quickly in terms of price."

As a result of those experiences, "I'm pretty sure we'll see a good entry price underneath the IPO for Anthropic by Memorial Day next year," Nearnberg said, citing sour sentiment toward technology and concern surrounding circular financing as short-term headwinds.

To be clear, all the retail investors CNBC spoke with are optimistic on AI as a transformative technology — even the ones sitting out the IPO. One trader said he believes in AI so much that he uses Anthropic's Claude, a conversational AI assistant, every day.

"From a price-to-sales ratio, Anthropic's like a really good deal," Faigen said. But "it's still a massive valuation for a $1.4 trillion company, and there are significantly better deals out there if you are a fundamental investor."

Faigen, who works for an agentic brokerage firm, said he's "leaning towards patience," as he believes Anthropic is going to be public "for a very long time."

"I just am not in a rush to get in on day one based off the valuation," he told CNBC.

Anthropic's valuation depends on its ability to sustain its rapid growth. In the nine months through Sept. 30, revenue reached more than $33 billion, 1,200% above the year-earlier period. Aggregate spending commitments total about $900 billion, primarily centered around computing power and infrastructure.

Breakneck growth leads plenty of retail and institutional investors to approach not just the Anthropic IPO but the entire AI trade with caution.

"A lot of it comes down to scrutiny over how much money they're spending on AI," said Bret Kenwell, U.S. investment analyst at eToro. "Investors want to see that those investments are turning into tangible returns, into tangible revenue."

#2746. Questions by position as Eagles return from 2026 bye week

Questions by position as Eagles return from 2026 bye week originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

This has been a miserable Eagles season and there are still eight games left.

The Eagles (2-7) finally had a chance to regroup during their Week 10 bye week but remaining the basement of the NFC East. They're not technically out of the division race, although The Athletic gives them less than a 1% chance to make the playoffs.

But there are still plenty of reasons to watch the rest of the season even if it ends in early January. With that in mind, here's a question about each position group as the Eagles return from their bye week:

Quarterback: How much better will Joe Milton get?

The Eagles put Joe Milton in a tough spot against the Giants in Week 9. While it's understandable that they hoped his mobility would help behind a struggling offensive line, Milton arrived just three weeks earlier and was still learning the offense. Heck, after the loss Milton said he told his OL that he vowed to learn their verbiage up front so they could communicate better. Maybe we see that in the second half of the season. Even though the Eagles lost to the Giants, Milton did some good things. He showed off his strong arm and used his legs at time. It'll be interesting to see if he can prove to at least be a backup-level QB going into 2027.

Running back: Will a healthy Saquon still be elite?

It was a frustrating first half of the season for Saquon Barkley, who has dealt with a stinger, a hamstring injury and a back injury. He was limited to just 69 carries in seven games for 296 yards and 3 touchdowns. Over the two games before the bye, Barkley had 26 carries for just 52 yards and 2 touchdowns. So his high yards-per-carry average plummeted to 4.3. But at times this season, Barkley has looked elite. The Eagles made it clear that they weren't going to trade Saquon because they think he's going to be a big part of the team after this season. We'll see if he got healthier during the bye week and if he can get back to his elite status.

Receiver: Is Makai Lemon going to continue to improve?

The Eagles were without DeVonta Smith (hamstring) and Dontayvion Wicks (ankle) for the three games leading into the bye week. The Eagles went 0-for-3 in those games and clearly missed their top two wideouts. But the silver lining in their absences was that rookie receiver Makai Lemon had the best performances of his young career:

Week 9: 6 for 61Week 8: 6 for 57Week 7: 7 for 67, 1 TD

The Eagles drafted Lemon with the No. 20 overall pick and after a slow start, he has started to produce. The Eagles are obviously going to get Smith and Wicks back in the lineup but they have to find a balance too — because it's so important that Lemon continues to grow.

Tight end: Will Eli Stowers get a bigger role?

The Eagles held on to Dallas Goedert at the trade deadline so he's obviously going to be their TE1 the rest of this season. But they should make a concerted effort to see more of second-round pick Eli Stowers. Stowers missed the first six games of the season on IR before making his debut against the Cowboys. But in three games, Stowers has played a grand total of just 32 snaps.

Here's how the TE snaps have looked the last three games:

Dallas Goedert: 160Johnny Mundt: 37Eli Stowers: 32Grant Calcaterra: 27

Earlier in the season, Mundt was playing way more, so it is at least a good sign that the coaching staff has backed off his usage. And it's obvious that Stowers isn't a finished product, especially as a blocker, but the Eagles should at least give him more opportunities.

The other side question for the tight end position: Will Zach Ertz get a chance to break the record?

Ertz is stuck on the practice squad with 582 receptions as an Eagle, just seven behind Harold Carmichael (589), who holds the record. In this lost season, it would be a real shame if the Eagles can't get Ertz to the 53-man roster for a run at that record.

Offensive line: Can Markel Bell and Drew Kendall prove themselves?

The Eagles' offensive line has struggled this season and was at one point in a complete state of flux. But we know as long as Drew Kendall and Markel Bell are healthy, they're going to be the starters at center and right tackle for the rest of the season. Kendall is a second-year player and Bell is a rookie, so if they play well there's a good chance they'll be long-term starters. Kendall got into the lineup early this season and has probably been playing at a higher level than they expected from Cam Jurgens, who was eventually traded to Baltimore. But Kendall hasn't proven that he's a long-term solution. And Bell has definitely had some struggles since taking over for Lane Johnson, who abruptly retired earlier in the season. Bell (6-9, 346) has given up 5 sacks and 19 pressures this season, per PFF. He has had some shaky moments, but some good ones too.

Defensive tackle: How much will the Eagles miss Jordan Davis?

The Eagles made a huge trade a week ago, when they dealt Jordan Davis to the Jacksonville Jaguars for a 2027 first-round pick and a 2029 second-round pick. That was tremendous value for Davis, but they will miss him on the field and in the locker room. Without Davis, the Eagles will need someone to play more early downs and will need other players to take up his overall snaps. We might see more Byron Young and Ty Robinson down the stretch. It'll also be worth watching how Jalen Carter handles this move. Carter and Davis are close and have been playing together for many years, dating back to their days at Georgia.

And will Moro Ojomo now get an extension? The Eagles haven't given out in-season extensions in recent years but that's because GM Howie Roseman was worried about dividing a locker room of a potential Super Bowl contender. That is definitely not a concern this season. And the Eagles should work to keep Ojomo beyond this season.

Edge rusher: Will Jonathan Greenard heat up?

Not only did the Eagles give up picks to acquire Jonathan Greenard, they also gave him a four-year deal worth $98 million. Greenard's time with the Eagles got off to a rough start when he suffered a pec injury before training camp that kept him out all summer and the beginning of the season. When he was finally back on the field and healthy for the first time, he then suffered an ankle injury he has had trouble getting over. Greenard has played in just six games this year and has just one sack and three QB hits. But if he's able to have a strong second half, that would be good news because his contract dictates that he's very likely going to be on the team for at least next season.

Linebacker: Will Vic Fangio keep the rotation?

The Eagles held on to Zack Baun at the trade deadline so we know he is going to be one of their starters the rest of the season. The Eagles had gone to a rotation at the other spot between Jihaad Campbell and Jeremiah Trotter Jr. But because Campbell missed the last couple games before the bye, Trotter got all the snaps. While it seems like Trotter has simply outplayed Campbell this season, there's a really good chance the Eagles go back to that rotation. They used a first-round pick on Campbell last year and really need to see what they have in him. Campbell has been disappointing through the first year and a half of his NFL career but he plays a position that can take some time to develop and the Eagles shouldn't give up on him yet. While Howie Roseman will probably want to see his first-round pick succeed, we'll have to see if Vic Fangio keeps giving Campbell playing time.

Cornerback: Can Cooper DeJean turn his season around?

One of the strangest storylines of the 2026 season has been the regression of Cooper DeJean. This guy was a key starter in the Super Bowl season as a rookie and was an All-Pro in Year 2. But Year 3 has been a struggle for DeJean and it goes beyond the snaps where he's playing from safety depth. He has also struggled in his normal nickel cornerback spot, which was extremely surprising. While Quinyon Mitchell has been excellent again in Year 3, DeJean has dropped off. Both are eligible for contract extensions after this season and Mitchell seems like a no-brainer. He's one of the best outside cornerbacks in the NFL and even though he doesn't have a ton of interceptions, it has been easy to see how good he is. But it might be tougher to pay DeJean if he doesn't show improvement. There was already the issue of how to value nickel corners and now DeJean is struggling.

Safety: Is Drew Mukuba a long-term starter?

The Eagles used a second-round pick on Drew Mukuba last year and it's time to see if he's really going to be a long-term starter in Philly. There's no doubt that Mukuba has some play-making ability but his play has been inconsistent in coverage and has struggled as a tackler. Maybe it really hurt his play when the Eagles allowed Reed Blankenship to leave in free agency. But Mukuba was given a full-time starting job this season and hasn't been good enough to think he's going to be in that spot for the next several years.

Specialists: Will Braden Mann be team MVP?

It's never a good sign when the bright spot of a season is the punter, but Braden Mann has been awesome. The 28-year-old punter has already had 49 punts with an average of 47.5 yards and a net average of 41.3 yards per punt. He had that monster 86-yard punt before the bye. But the stat that stands out most is his punts inside the 20. Mann has already downed 22 punts inside the 20, which accounts for 44.9% of his punts this season. He has just two touchbacks.

Coaching: Will Nick Sirianni survive this season?

This season has been ugly. The Eagles won their first two games and have now lost seven straight. Sure, they lost Jalen Hurts for the season and have dealt with countless other injuries, so it has been a weird year. Maybe Jeffrey Lurie looks at the situation and gives Sirianni a mulligan. After all, Sirianni has been to the playoffs five times, to the Super Bowl twice and won it all to cap the 2024 season. That wasn't that long ago. But we have also seen Lurie fire a Super Bowl-winning head coach and explain that it wasn't because Doug Pederson deserved to get fired; he moved on in 2021 because he thought it was in the best interest of the franchise. So Lurie has to look at this situation and determine if the best thing for the franchise is to run it back with Sirianni in 2027. And if he does that, who are his coordinators? It's hard to imagine Sirianni getting fired before the end of the season, so those are all fair questions for January. In the interim, let's just see if the players continue to fight for Sirianni and his future.

#2743. Anthropic IPO: AI lab considers increasing the offering as investor demand surges

Anthropic is considering upsizing its IPO, people familiar with the details told CNBC, as the artificial intelligence lab weighs setting the price well above $80.

The company closed its order books and locked in total demand on Tuesday, setting the stage for the Claude creator's eagerly awaited Nasdaq listing. Institutional demand for the offering is around $400 billion, while retail demand is approaching $100 billion, according to the people, who asked not to be named because they were not authorized to speak publicly.

The orders are indications of interest and are not binding. All of the details are subject to change, and Anthropic will make final decisions on Wednesday afternoon, the people said. Anthropic is expected to set its final IPO price after market close on Wednesday.

Demand for the offering is higher than it was for Elon Musk's SpaceX, even though the rocket maker had a bigger market offering. SpaceX debuted on the Nasdaq in June in by far the biggest IPO on record, ultimately raising $85.7 billion.

The AI lab said last week that it would offer 825 million shares in the IPO at a price range between $70 and $78 per share, which would net up to $64.35 billion at the high end of that range.

A representative for Anthropic declined to comment.

Anthropic's listing is a major test for the AI industry, which has experienced a set of recent shocks, including a dramatic backlash that was felt at the polls in the midterm elections and last week's ouster of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. The market was already jittery on concerns that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for many data center developers and neoclouds.

Tech's biggest companies have significant exposure to Anthropic, whether as an investor, partner or customer. Amazon and Google both own stakes valued at over $100 billion, and a group of tech companies, including Google, Microsoft, Nvidia and Advanced Micro Devices agreed to collectively invest $26 billion in the IPO. Anthropic, meanwhile, is a buyer of costly systems from Nvidia, Google, Amazon and AMD.

"I think if we do this right, we can create an age of miracles where humanity is able to solve problems that have eluded us for millennia," Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei said in Anthropic's roadshow video. "We have to make sure that all the risks, all the ways that things can go wrong with this technology, we very carefully address those risks as we bring the benefits and as we have a viable business at the same time."

Amodei co-founded Anthropic alongside a handful of other researchers less than six years ago, after the group collectively defected from OpenAI. The company is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and it primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

Anthropic has been racing to maintain an edge over OpenAI and Google in the enterprise market, and it experienced a period of explosive growth in 2026, driven largely by the popularity of its AI coding tool, Claude Code. OpenAI has confidentially filed for an IPO and has said a debut is likely in 2027.

For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Anthropic said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue, up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year, according to its prospectus. The company reported a $104 billion net loss during the nine-month period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic lost $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025, the filing said.

Anthropic said in an amended filing that its run-rate revenue was roughly $81 billion at the end of October, up $10 billion from the previous month.

The company has faced plenty of challenges during its meteoric rise over its positions on safety, regulation and national security, leading to two high-profile disputes with the U.S. government this year. Its prospectus included more than 90 pages of risk factors for investors to consider ahead of its IPO.

"Our aim is to help humankind chart a safe course through this technological evolution, open new frontiers of discovery, and bring forth an age of miracles," Anthropic's co-founders wrote in the company's prospectus. "Everyone who owns our stock, whether an institution or an individual, becomes part of that effort."

#2742. Eagles continue reshaping roster by reportedly releasing veteran wide receiver

Eagles continue reshaping roster by reportedly releasing veteran wide receiver originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Hollywood Brown's brief and odd Eagles career is over after four games and five catches.

The Eagles are releasing Brown by mutual consent of the player and the team, Jordan Schultz of the Scultz Report posted on social media Thursday night.

The Eagles signed Brown to a one-year, $5 million contract in March with the expectation that he'd be a significant part of the Eagles' passing game following the departure of A.J. Brown. But even with Brown gone and DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks both hurt much of the season, Brown has been an afterthought.

He's only played in four of the Eagles' nine games, getting nine targets and catching five passes for 63 yards along with two rushing attempts for 12 yards. His longest catch was a 19-yarder from Tanner McKee in the loss to the Cowboys. He had a season-high 30 yards in that game.

Brown expressed frustration with his limited role in a recent conversation with NBC Sports Philadelphia:

"Of course, you want to be somewhere where you play, but at the ultimate end of the day, it's not my plan. So I might want to play, I might want to contribute, but if that's not the plan for me, I can't dwell on it too much or you'll be unhappy. And then you're taking that back home. I can't take that back home to the wife and kids. So I gotta try to stay as positive as I can."

Asked how that was going, he laughed and said, "Some days better than others."

He added: "We're all dealing with something and we're dealing with something as a team. So I don't wanna add that extra stress."

Brown said he expected to play more but was dealing with it because he didn't want to put personal issues ahead of team needs.

"I did (expect a bigger role), but I do understand stuff happens," he said. "I mean, you can't take anything personal in this business, so you kind of just got to go about your process and when that time happens, whatever happens, you've just got to be ready for it."

Brown, a 1st-round pick in 2019, had a 1,000-yard season for the Ravens in 2021 and over 700 yards in both 2020 and 2022. He was 49-for-587 with five touchdowns last year. In his career, he has 378 catches for 4,385 yards and 33 touchdowns.

The Eagles, 2-7, have lost seven straight game. Their offense is ranked 32nd in the NFL in yards per game, 30th in points per game and 29th in passing yards per game.

With Brown no longer on the roster, the Eagles have rookie 1st-round pick Makai Lemon, 2nd-year undrafted free agent Darius Cooper and Elijah Moore as healthy wide receivers. Smith and Wicks have both missed the last two games but could be back after the bye week.

Lemon is the Eagles' leading receiver with 37-for-312 and a touchdown and Smith (26-310-2), Wicks (19-284-1) and Cooper (13-200-4) are next.

The Eagles have two WRs on the practice squad – Erik Ezukanma and Xavier Gipson.

Because it's after the trade deadline, Brown is subject to the waiver claim process despite being a vested veteran with seven years of pension credit.

#2740. Nvidia’s Kress faces a divided workforce as some employees push for Huang’s return

The day before Colette Kress led her first earnings call as Nvidia's interim CEO, she did not want to talk about her predecessor, Jensen Huang.

"This is a new era for Nvidia," she told employees in an internal meeting on Monday, according to a person present, who spoke on the condition of anonymity. When someone brought up Huang, Kress quickly changed the subject, according to the person.

On Wednesday, Kress is due to address the whole company at the first Nvidia all-hands meeting since the public chaos over Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy and other reported relationships. And while Kress will be tasked with filling the leadership vacuum created by Huang's departure, she is also expected to tamp down public calls to bring him back.

Huang himself weighed in with his first public statement Tuesday saying he would be "honored" to return to Nvidia.

The interim CEO, who has served as the company's CFO since 2013, must steady employees and convince customers and investors that Nvidia can execute on its ambitious targets without the man who made them. For those at the top, that has meant making clear that the company does not plan to bring Huang back.

"The board has determined that they will not reinstate Jensen," Kress said in an interview with CNBC's Jim Cramer on Tuesday. "We want to grow in terms of everything that we need to do with our employees, our customers, our shareholders, and that is where we are going to focus moving forward."

Harvey Jones, the newly appointed chair of Nvidia's board, echoed the sentiment on Tuesday's earnings call. "The Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO," he said in opening remarks.

Nvidia declined to comment for this story.

Kress stressed on the earnings call that the company was focused on the workers who remain, who she said are "committed to doing their life's work at Nvidia." The company also reported better-than-expected earnings and seemed to calm investors' nerves, at least for the moment.

Some employees are telling a different story. Support for Huang's return is growing inside Nvidia, sources tell CNBC. An online petition circulating since the weekend has nearly 200,000 signatures, with more than 10,000 of them claiming to be current Nvidia employees. Nvidia currently has roughly 42,000 employees.

Outside Nvidia, Huang's reinstatement has been called for by at least two major investors and tech heavyweights such as Elon Musk, Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney. President Donald Trump said he "would not mind" seeing Huang return as CEO.

Several employees posted on X over the last few days, voicing support for Huang and calling for his return. One robotics engineer said the founder's departure led to his own decision to leave.

Another senior Nvidia employee, Nader Khalil, sent a letter to the company's board and top executives warning that failing to reinstate Huang could lead to a loss of "irreplaceable talent," according to The Wall Street Journal.

Huang was known for his deep day-to-day involvement in his company's operations, often responding directly to engineers and chiming in personally on technical projects.

Engineering employees, in particular, were "extremely devoted to him, almost like a demigod," according to a former Nvidia employee who was at the company for more than 15 years and spoke to CNBC on condition of anonymity.

Wednesday's all-hands is a standard meeting: Nvidia traditionally holds one on the morning after each quarterly earnings call, according to the former employee.

Still, the meeting will be unlike anything the company has experienced. Employees tell CNBC that they plan to share their grievances at the all-hands, though it's still unclear if they will be allowed to ask questions. Kress will need to direct attention toward the business and away from Huang's return.

Huang took time to address employee questions during previous post-earnings all-hands meetings, the former Nvidia employee told CNBC, adding that Kress will be "eager to demonstrate there's a new sheriff in town."

Kress ended Tuesday's earnings call with an upbeat message: "Our customers and our partners are with us. And employees are committed. We've got this."

Wednesday's all-hands meeting could determine if employees agree.

— CNBC's Lora Kolodny contributed to this report.

#2738. Fed’s Waller says central bank may need to ‘shock people’ with half-point rate hike

Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller on Wednesday called for aggressive action against inflation, saying the central bank needs to "shock people" as prices continue to rise more than he expected.

In the near term, the policymaker told CNBC in a live interview that a half percentage point increase in the Fed's benchmark rate is probably necessary at the Dec. 8-9 meeting of the Federal Open Market Committee.

He said the central bank needs to reverse the three quarter-point reductions from last year and may need to do more.

"I'm very, very concerned about inflation. It's kind of headed in the wrong direction, and it's spreading out," Waller told CNBC's Steve Liesman during a "Squawk Box" interview. "If you really want to get it down, you've got to start taking more aggressive actions."

"You might need to, like, shock people," he said.

Futures prices are fully pricing in a quarter percentage point hike at the FOMC's December meeting. They also are anticipating another move in January, followed by at least one additional increase before the end of 2027, according to the CME Group's FedWatch gauge.

Waller said the Fed wouldn't necessarily have to approve a half-point hike in December if it raised rates by a quarter point and signaled another move coming in January. However, Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh has been against that kind of forward guidance on grounds that it ties policymakers' hands.

"If you're not going to signal this very clearly to markets, you're going in January, my view is then you have to go 50 [basis points] in December to put it in place and get it done," he said.

Recent statements from other central bankers indicate a range of opinions, though most agree that more policy tightening is needed to bring down inflation.

In a speech Tuesday, St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem suggested as many as four hikes by the spring of 2027. Some Fed officials, including regional Presidents Beth Hammack of Cleveland and Lorie Logan of Dallas, have endorsed more gradual tightening.

However, continued inflation above the Fed's 2% target might push officials into a more aggressive stance.

The FOMC approved its first interest rate hike in more than three years in September, and then held rates steady at its October meeting. While inflation has continued to run hot since then, other Fed officials seem less eager to drop the policy hammer as heavily as Waller.

"I don't think the situation calls for dramatic action," New York Fed President John Williams said Tuesday in a conversation with Axios. "I think what we've been doing over the last past few months is ... working well. We're collecting the data, analyzing it, having the debate and making a decision."

The Fed in 2025 approved three rate cuts amid worries over the labor market and the belief that inflation would continue to moderate toward the central bank's 2% goal.

However, inflation reignited earlier this year after the strikes the U.S. and Israel launched against Iran in late February. Energy prices spiked while tariff impacts lingered, keeping most inflation readings above 3%.

"First of all, you've got to take back the three insurance cuts we put in last year," Waller said. "If the policy rate last summer in 2025 wasn't bringing inflation down, getting back to that isn't going to bring it down. So we may have to go above ... three hikes plus maybe some more to get it to start actually coming back down."

The FOMC later Wednesday will release minutes from the October meeting.

#2737. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

Castle will probably draw the defensive assignment on Cade, and he will have to be careful to stay out of foul trouble. With Victor Wembanyama creating a zone of exclusion1 in the paint, the Silver and Black have a good shot of limiting the damage from Detroit's superstar.

De'Aaron Fox was out for the Jazz game, but he's not listed on today's injury report, so he should be back in the starting lineup. Fox's steadiness has been a big factor in the Spurs' win streak, and his absence in Utah could have been part of the reason that the Jazz were able to keep the game close until a late fourth quarter surge from the Silver and Black.

If the Spurs can extend the streak to 13 with a win tonight, they have a good shot at making it last even longer, since the next three games are against the Pelicans, Clippers, and Kings, which are all near the bottom of the Western Conference standings. In the way too early to matter playoff positioning race, the Spurs can keep pac with the Lakers for first place tonight with a win, or even take sole possession of first with a Lakers loss to the Hornets. There are a ton of different viewing options tonight, so if you don't like the food segment that Sean and Jacob do at the start of the third quarter, you might want to watch on Peacock. I used to hate that part of the show, but it's kind of growing on me as the win streak keeps getting longer. Let's Go SPURS, and Stephon must dominate Cade.

There's a concept of players having gravity with their ability to score and draw defenders, creating open shots for their teammates. Victor has something else on defense, the ability to deny opponents the opportunity or even the desire to take shots anywhere near the paint. I'm going to call this anti-gravity, because he's an alien.

There's an extended delay in the third quarter caused by a food spill from the Spurs broadcasting team's table at courtside.

#2734. ESPN chief: Media coverage of Joe Buck, Troy Aikman dustup is "disappointing"

The Joe Buck vs. Troy Aikman Real Broadcasters of the NFL drama has added another layer of unexpected reality to the ultimate reality show that is pro football. Their mutual boss claims that ESPN doesn’t like the coverage of the situation.

“Joe and Troy have done a great job for us on Monday Night Football.” ESPN president Jimmy Pitaro said Tuesday at the SBJ Media Innovators conference, via Brandon Contes of Awful Announcing. “When I say ‘us,’ they’ve done a great job for ESPN, they’ve done a great job for the league, they’ve done a great job for the game, and most importantly, they’ve done a great job for fans.

“Are we happy about the media coverage recently? Of course not. It’s been disappointing. But these guys are professionals, and they’re delivering every week. If you watched it last night, another super high-quality production and telecast, and I’d fully expect that to continue that through the season and through the Super Bowl.”

At some level, they shouldn’t be disappointed. Any attention is, at some level, good attention. While it’s unlikely that anyone is tuning in for a Monday night game solely to witness the interactions of Buck and Aikman, it adds a little extra something to the overall buzz arising from the broadcast.

The deeper question is who Pitaro and the other big shots at ESPN are “disappointed” with. The situation didn’t come to light because everyone involved decided to keep the dirty laundry in house. People started talking privately. Aikman has said plenty publicly that confirms the situation and otherwise stirs the pot.

ESPN has no cause to be disappointed with those who did their jobs by pulling the threads of a genuinely interesting, uncontived, and in many ways unprecedented story. If the folks who write the checks should be miffed at anyone, it should be at the recipients of those checks who decided not to hold their own tongues in check regarding an obviously sensitive situation.

#2731. Chiefs sign RB E.J. Smith, Emmitt's son, to the practice squad

The Chiefs signed running back E.J. Smith to their practice squad on Tuesday, according to the NFL’s personnel notice.

Smith is the son of the NFL’s all-time leading rusher, Emmitt Smith.

The Texas A&M product signed with the Chiefs as an undrafted free agent after the 2026 draft. He spent the offseason and training camp with the team but was waived with an injury designation Sept. 4.

He had remained a free agent until Tuesday.

Smith spent four seasons at Stanford and two at Texas A&M. He finished his career with 207 carries for 969 yards and nine touchdowns and added 470 receiving yards and a touchdown.

Smith does not have a jersey number yet on the Chiefs’ roster, but he won’t wear his father’s No. 22. Rookie defensive back Jadon Canady has that number in Kansas City. Smith wore No. 35 in training camp, but practice squad running back Nate Carter has that number.

The Chiefs also signed tight end John FitzPatrick to the practice squad. They terminated the practice squad contracts of wide receivers Jacob DeJesus and Bub Means.

#2730. Dan Campbell won't wallow in "what ifs" arising from injuries

The Lions have, in recent weeks, lost both linebacker Jack Campbell (neck) and defensive end Aidan Hutchinson (knee) to significant injuries. Coach Dan Campbell won’t wallow in worrying about what may have been if the injuries hadn’t occurred.

“I think there’s times where, if you’re not careful, you lay in bed at night and you start thinking about all the ‘what ifs,’” Campbell told reporters on Tuesday. “And at some point, you just become a miserable piece of shit. And I’m not gonna do it, man. We’re not doing it.

“I refuse to look at it that way. Does it sting? Yeah, it sure did in the moment. It’s time to move on. We have to.

“And what has happened with Jack Campbell and with Hutch now will not be in vain. We can’t allow it to. They got us to this point. And so what are we gonna do about it, man? Are we just gonna let it go to waste, or are we gonna do something and pick up the slack?”

It’s the right attitude. Injuries happen. Sometimes, they happen to great players. (The more great players a team has, the greater the chances that one of them will be injured.)

The only approach is to tap the next man up on the shoulder, expect him to do his job to the best of his ability, not allow it to become a “woe is us” situation, and keep going.

That becomes easier, of course, if a team has quality depth. Which is why some teams that are top heavy with highly-paid stars find themselves in a pinch when those stars aren’t available to play.
